Chapter 1: Arithmetic Operations
This chapter focuses on fundamental arithmetic operations including addition, subtraction,
multiplication, and division. Students will practice these operations through engaging story problems
that relate to real-world situations.
Grade 1 Problems
Problem 1: Olivia has 6 stickers. They give 1 stickers to a friend. How many stickers does Olivia
have left?
Answer: 5
Explanation: Olivia started with 6 stickers. After giving away 1: 6 - 1 = 5 stickers.
Problem 2: Jack has 7 apples. Their friend gives them 3 more apples. How many apples does Jack
have now?
Answer: 10
Explanation: Jack started with 7 apples. Adding 3 more: 7 + 3 = 10 apples.
Problem 3: Emily has 6 apples. Their friend gives them 4 more apples. How many apples does Emily
have now?
Answer: 10
Explanation: Emily started with 6 apples. Adding 4 more: 6 + 4 = 10 apples.
Problem 4: Benjamin has 12 stickers. They give 5 stickers to a friend. How many stickers does
Benjamin have left?
Answer: 7
Explanation: Benjamin started with 12 stickers. After giving away 5: 12 - 5 = 7 stickers.
Problem 5: Aiden has 14 stickers. They give 3 stickers to a friend. How many stickers does Aiden
have left?
Answer: 11
Explanation: Aiden started with 14 stickers. After giving away 3: 14 - 3 = 11 stickers.

Problem 6: Samuel has 12 stickers. They give 1 stickers to a friend. How many stickers does Samuel

Challenge Problems - Grade 1
These advanced problems require multi-step thinking and deeper understanding.
Challenge 1: Samuel has 8 apples. Their friend gives them 6 more apples. How many apples does
Samuel have now?
Answer: 14
Solution: Samuel started with 8 apples. Adding 6 more: 8 + 6 = 14 apples.

Grade 2 Problems
Problem 1: Amelia has 10 boxes of crayons. Each box has 4 crayons. How many crayons does
Amelia have in total?
Answer: 40
Explanation: Multiplying boxes by crayons per box: 10 × 4 = 40 crayons.
Problem 2: Alexander collected 48 seashells on Monday and 12 seashells on Tuesday. How many
seashells did Alexander collect in total?
Answer: 60
Explanation: Adding the seashells from both days: 48 + 12 = 60 seashells.

Problem 3: Charlotte collected 37 seashells on Monday and 27 seashells on Tuesday. How many
seashells did Charlotte collect in total?
Answer: 64
Explanation: Adding the seashells from both days: 37 + 27 = 64 seashells.
Problem 4: Mia has 10 boxes of crayons. Each box has 5 crayons. How many crayons does Mia
have in total?
Answer: 50
Explanation: Multiplying boxes by crayons per box: 10 × 5 = 50 crayons.
Problem 5: Mason has 3 boxes of crayons. Each box has 2 crayons. How many crayons does
Mason have in total?
Answer: 6
Explanation: Multiplying boxes by crayons per box: 3 × 2 = 6 crayons.
Problem 6: Mason has 9 boxes of crayons. Each box has 3 crayons. How many crayons does
Mason have in total?
Answer: 27
Explanation: Multiplying boxes by crayons per box: 9 × 3 = 27 crayons.
Problem 7: Lucas has 7 boxes of crayons. Each box has 2 crayons. How many crayons does Lucas
have in total?
Answer: 14
Explanation: Multiplying boxes by crayons per box: 7 × 2 = 14 crayons.
Problem 8: Emily had 95 candies. They ate 19 candies. How many candies does Emily have left?
Answer: 76
Explanation: Subtracting the candies eaten: 95 - 19 = 76 candies.
Problem 9: Aiden has 5 boxes of crayons. Each box has 3 crayons. How many crayons does Aiden
have in total?
Answer: 15
Explanation: Multiplying boxes by crayons per box: 5 × 3 = 15 crayons.

Problem 10: Emily has 9 boxes of crayons. Each box has 5 crayons. How many crayons does Emily

Challenge Problems - Grade 2
These advanced problems require multi-step thinking and deeper understanding.
Challenge 1: Mason collected 46 seashells on Monday and 15 seashells on Tuesday. How many
seashells did Mason collect in total?
Answer: 61
Solution: Adding the seashells from both days: 46 + 15 = 61 seashells.
Challenge 2: Noah collected 16 seashells on Monday and 34 seashells on Tuesday. How many
seashells did Noah collect in total?
Answer: 50
Solution: Adding the seashells from both days: 16 + 34 = 50 seashells.
Challenge 3: Olivia had 73 candies. They ate 28 candies. How many candies does Olivia have left?
Answer: 45
Solution: Subtracting the candies eaten: 73 - 28 = 45 candies.
Challenge 4: Evelyn collected 26 seashells on Monday and 27 seashells on Tuesday. How many
seashells did Evelyn collect in total?

Answer: 53
Solution: Adding the seashells from both days: 26 + 27 = 53 seashells.
Challenge 5: Samuel had 41 candies. They ate 26 candies. How many candies does Samuel have
left?
Answer: 15
Solution: Subtracting the candies eaten: 41 - 26 = 15 candies.
Challenge 6: Sebastian has 10 boxes of crayons. Each box has 5 crayons. How many crayons does
Sebastian have in total?
Answer: 50
Solution: Multiplying boxes by crayons per box: 10 × 5 = 50 crayons.
Challenge 7: Olivia had 57 candies. They ate 14 candies. How many candies does Olivia have left?
Answer: 43
Solution: Subtracting the candies eaten: 57 - 14 = 43 candies.
Challenge 8: Ethan had 63 candies. They ate 26 candies. How many candies does Ethan have left?
Answer: 37
Solution: Subtracting the candies eaten: 63 - 26 = 37 candies.
Challenge 9: Ava had 34 candies. They ate 26 candies. How many candies does Ava have left?
Answer: 8
Solution: Subtracting the candies eaten: 34 - 26 = 8 candies.
Challenge 10: Avery had 59 candies. They ate 18 candies. How many candies does Avery have left?
Answer: 41
Solution: Subtracting the candies eaten: 59 - 18 = 41 candies.
Challenge 11: Samuel collected 12 seashells on Monday and 15 seashells on Tuesday. How many
seashells did Samuel collect in total?
Answer: 27
Solution: Adding the seashells from both days: 12 + 15 = 27 seashells.

Challenge 12: Elizabeth had 49 candies. They ate 19 candies. How many candies does Elizabeth
have left?
Answer: 30
Solution: Subtracting the candies eaten: 49 - 19 = 30 candies.
Challenge 13: Emily had 91 candies. They ate 27 candies. How many candies does Emily have left?
Answer: 64
Solution: Subtracting the candies eaten: 91 - 27 = 64 candies.
Challenge 14: Aiden had 44 candies. They ate 24 candies. How many candies does Aiden have left?
Answer: 20
Solution: Subtracting the candies eaten: 44 - 24 = 20 candies.
Challenge 15: Henry had 69 candies. They ate 26 candies. How many candies does Henry have
left?
Answer: 43
Solution: Subtracting the candies eaten: 69 - 26 = 43 candies.
Grade 3 Problems
Problem 1: Mia buys 48 packs of trading cards. Each pack has 12 cards. How many cards does Mia
have?
Answer: 576
Explanation: Multiplying packs by cards per pack: 48 × 12 = 576 cards.
Problem 2: A library has 312 fiction books and 135 non-fiction books. How many books does the
library have in total?
Answer: 447
Explanation: Adding fiction and non-fiction books: 312 + 135 = 447 books.

Problem 3: A library has 470 fiction books and 469 non-fiction books. How many books does the
library have in total?
Answer: 939
Explanation: Adding fiction and non-fiction books: 470 + 469 = 939 books.
Problem 4: Aiden has 165 cookies to share equally among 11 friends. How many cookies does each
friend get?
Answer: 15
Explanation: Dividing cookies equally: 165 ÷ 11 = 15 cookies per friend.
Problem 5: Sebastian has 77 cookies to share equally among 7 friends. How many cookies does
each friend get?
Answer: 11
Explanation: Dividing cookies equally: 77 ÷ 7 = 11 cookies per friend.
Problem 6: A library has 264 fiction books and 419 non-fiction books. How many books does the
library have in total?
Answer: 683
Explanation: Adding fiction and non-fiction books: 264 + 419 = 683 books.

library have in total?
Answer: 686

Grade 4 Problems
Problem 1: A factory produces 84 toys each day. How many toys are produced in 48 days?
Answer: 4032
Explanation: Multiplying daily production by days: 84 × 48 = 4032 toys.
Problem 2: A store sold 585 items in January, 1754 items in February, and 1488 items in March.
How many items were sold in total?
Answer: 3827
Explanation: Adding all three months: 585 + 1754 + 1488 = 3827 items.
Problem 3: A factory produces 46 toys each day. How many toys are produced in 49 days?
Answer: 2254
Explanation: Multiplying daily production by days: 46 × 49 = 2254 toys.
Problem 4: A factory produces 37 toys each day. How many toys are produced in 30 days?
Answer: 1110
Explanation: Multiplying daily production by days: 37 × 30 = 1110 toys.
Problem 5: A city has a population of 9317 people. 2310 people moved away. What is the new
population?
Answer: 7007
Explanation: Subtracting people who moved: 9317 - 2310 = 7007 people.
Problem 6: A factory produces 80 toys each day. How many toys are produced in 16 days?
Answer: 1280
Explanation: Multiplying daily production by days: 80 × 16 = 1280 toys.
Problem 7: A farmer has 1080 eggs to pack into cartons. Each carton holds 24 eggs. How many
cartons can be filled?
Answer: 45

Explanation: Dividing eggs by carton size: 1080 ÷ 24 = 45 cartons.
Problem 8: A store sold 1363 items in January, 1043 items in February, and 1646 items in March.
How many items were sold in total?
Answer: 4052
Explanation: Adding all three months: 1363 + 1043 + 1646 = 4052 items.
Problem 9: A store sold 1532 items in January, 1656 items in February, and 632 items in March.
How many items were sold in total?
Answer: 3820
Explanation: Adding all three months: 1532 + 1656 + 632 = 3820 items.

Answer: 1863
Explanation: Multiplying daily production by days: 69 × 27 = 1863 toys.

cartons can be filled?
Answer: 37

Challenge Problems - Grade 4
These advanced problems require multi-step thinking and deeper understanding.
Challenge 1: A factory produces 28 toys each day. How many toys are produced in 25 days?
Answer: 700
Solution: Multiplying daily production by days: 28 × 25 = 700 toys.
Challenge 2: A farmer has 462 eggs to pack into cartons. Each carton holds 22 eggs. How many
cartons can be filled?
Answer: 21
Solution: Dividing eggs by carton size: 462 ÷ 22 = 21 cartons.

Challenge 3: A city has a population of 8147 people. 2770 people moved away. What is the new
population?
Answer: 5377
Solution: Subtracting people who moved: 8147 - 2770 = 5377 people.
Challenge 4: A store sold 545 items in January, 1931 items in February, and 1130 items in March.
How many items were sold in total?
Answer: 3606
Solution: Adding all three months: 545 + 1931 + 1130 = 3606 items.
Challenge 5: A factory produces 84 toys each day. How many toys are produced in 16 days?
Answer: 1344
Solution: Multiplying daily production by days: 84 × 16 = 1344 toys.
Challenge 6: A city has a population of 7314 people. 4698 people moved away. What is the new
population?
Answer: 2616
Solution: Subtracting people who moved: 7314 - 4698 = 2616 people.
Challenge 7: A farmer has 880 eggs to pack into cartons. Each carton holds 22 eggs. How many
cartons can be filled?
Answer: 40
Solution: Dividing eggs by carton size: 880 ÷ 22 = 40 cartons.

Answer: 1020

Challenge 9: A city has a population of 7434 people. 4667 people moved away. What is the new

Answer: 2767
Solution: Subtracting people who moved: 7434 - 4667 = 2767 people.

Challenge 10: A farmer has 384 eggs to pack into cartons. Each carton holds 16 eggs. How many

Answer: 24
Solution: Dividing eggs by carton size: 384 ÷ 16 = 24 cartons.
Challenge 11: A factory produces 99 toys each day. How many toys are produced in 43 days?
Answer: 4257

Answer: 1287

Grade 6 Problems
Problem 1: A teacher orders 3 pencils for each of 28 students, plus 9 extra pencils. How many
pencils in total?
Answer: 93
Explanation: First multiply: 28 × 3 = 84. Then add extra: 84 + 9 = 93 pencils.
Problem 2: The high temperature today is 8°C. The low temperature is -11°C. What is the difference
between the high and low temperatures?
Answer: 19
Explanation: Difference: 8 - (-11) = 8 + 11 = 19°C.

Answer: 89
Explanation: 10% of $98 is $9. Sale price: $98 - $9 = $89.

difference between the high and low temperatures?
Answer: 21
Explanation: Difference: 12 - (-9) = 12 + 9 = 21°C.
Problem 42: A jacket costs $164. It's on sale for 20% off. What is the sale price?
Answer: 132
Explanation: 20% of $164 is $32. Sale price: $164 - $32 = $132.
Problem 43: A teacher orders 8 pencils for each of 30 students, plus 12 extra pencils. How many
pencils in total?
Answer: 252
Explanation: First multiply: 30 × 8 = 240. Then add extra: 240 + 12 = 252 pencils.
Problem 44: A teacher orders 3 pencils for each of 30 students, plus 11 extra pencils. How many
pencils in total?
Answer: 101
Explanation: First multiply: 30 × 3 = 90. Then add extra: 90 + 11 = 101 pencils.
Problem 45: A jacket costs $149. It's on sale for 50% off. What is the sale price?
Answer: 75
Explanation: 50% of $149 is $74. Sale price: $149 - $74 = $75.

Challenge Problems - Grade 6
These advanced problems require multi-step thinking and deeper understanding.
Challenge 1: The high temperature today is 6°C. The low temperature is -9°C. What is the difference
between the high and low temperatures?

Answer: 15

Chapter 2: Fractions and Decimals
Fractions are an essential part of mathematics. This chapter introduces students to understanding,
comparing, adding, subtracting, multiplying, and dividing fractions through practical story-based
problems.
Grade 3 Problems
Problem 1: Liam has 6 cookies. They eat 2/3 of the cookies. How many cookies did Liam eat?
Answer: 4
Explanation: 2/3 of 6 = (2 × 6) ÷ 3 = 4 cookies.
Problem 2: Lucas has 6 cookies. They eat 1/2 of the cookies. How many cookies did Lucas eat?
Answer: 3
Explanation: 1/2 of 6 = (1 × 6) ÷ 2 = 3 cookies.
Problem 3: Joseph has 10 cookies. They eat 1/2 of the cookies. How many cookies did Joseph eat?
Answer: 5
Explanation: 1/2 of 10 = (1 × 10) ÷ 2 = 5 cookies.

Grade 4 Problems

Problem 1: Liam walks 5/8 of a mile to school and 2/8 of a mile back home. How far does Liam walk
in total?
Answer: 7/8
Explanation: Adding fractions with same denominator: 5/8 + 2/8 = 7/8 miles.
Problem 2: Jack walks 2/4 of a mile to school and 1/4 of a mile back home. How far does Jack walk
in total?
Answer: 3/4
Explanation: Adding fractions with same denominator: 2/4 + 1/4 = 3/4 miles.

Grade 5 Problems
Problem 1: Avery used 1/6 of a cup of flour for cookies and 2/3 of a cup for a cake. How much flour
was used in total?
Answer: 5/6
Explanation: Converting to common denominator 6: 1/6 = 1/6 and 2/3 = 4/6. Adding: 1/6 + 4/6 = 5/6 cups.
Problem 2: Sofia used 2/5 of a cup of flour for cookies and 3/6 of a cup for a cake. How much flour
was used in total?
Answer: 27/30
Explanation: Converting to common denominator 30: 2/5 = 12/30 and 3/6 = 15/30. Adding: 12/30 + 15/30
= 27/30 cups.
Problem 3: Avery used 1/5 of a cup of flour for cookies and 1/4 of a cup for a cake. How much flour
was used in total?
Answer: 9/20
Explanation: Converting to common denominator 20: 1/5 = 4/20 and 1/4 = 5/20. Adding: 4/20 + 5/20 =
9/20 cups.
Problem 4: Evelyn used 2/6 of a cup of flour for cookies and 4/7 of a cup for a cake. How much flour
was used in total?

Answer: 38/42
Explanation: Converting to common denominator 42: 2/6 = 14/42 and 4/7 = 24/42. Adding: 14/42 + 24/42
= 38/42 cups.
Problem 5: Samuel used 1/5 of a cup of flour for cookies and 5/6 of a cup for a cake. How much flour
was used in total?
Answer: 31/30
Explanation: Converting to common denominator 30: 1/5 = 6/30 and 5/6 = 25/30. Adding: 6/30 + 25/30 =
31/30 cups.
Problem 6: Henry used 1/2 of a cup of flour for cookies and 2/3 of a cup for a cake. How much flour

Answer: 7/6
Explanation: Converting to common denominator 6: 1/2 = 3/6 and 2/3 = 4/6. Adding: 3/6 + 4/6 = 7/6 cups.

Answer: 5/6
Explanation: Converting to common denominator 6: 1/3 = 2/6 and 1/2 = 3/6. Adding: 2/6 + 3/6 = 5/6 cups.

14/12 cups.

23/20 cups.

Solution: Converting to common denominator 4: 2/4 = 2/4 and 1/2 = 2/4. Adding: 2/4 + 2/4 = 4/4 cups.
Challenge 3: Liam used 1/3 of a cup of flour for cookies and 1/2 of a cup for a cake. How much flour
was used in total?
Answer: 5/6
Solution: Converting to common denominator 6: 1/3 = 2/6 and 1/2 = 3/6. Adding: 2/6 + 3/6 = 5/6 cups.
Challenge 4: Benjamin used 1/4 of a cup of flour for cookies and 1/2 of a cup for a cake. How much
flour was used in total?
Answer: 3/4
Solution: Converting to common denominator 4: 1/4 = 1/4 and 1/2 = 2/4. Adding: 1/4 + 2/4 = 3/4 cups.
Challenge 5: Alexander used 1/4 of a cup of flour for cookies and 1/3 of a cup for a cake. How much
flour was used in total?
Answer: 7/12
Solution: Converting to common denominator 12: 1/4 = 3/12 and 1/3 = 4/12. Adding: 3/12 + 4/12 = 7/12
cups.
Challenge 6: Liam used 2/6 of a cup of flour for cookies and 5/7 of a cup for a cake. How much flour
was used in total?
Answer: 44/42

Solution: Converting to common denominator 42: 2/6 = 14/42 and 5/7 = 30/42. Adding: 14/42 + 30/42 =
44/42 cups.
Challenge 7: Sofia used 2/3 of a cup of flour for cookies and 2/6 of a cup for a cake. How much flour
was used in total?
Answer: 6/6
Solution: Converting to common denominator 6: 2/3 = 4/6 and 2/6 = 2/6. Adding: 4/6 + 2/6 = 6/6 cups.

was used in total?
Answer: 7/12
Solution: Converting to common denominator 12: 1/3 = 4/12 and 1/4 = 3/12. Adding: 4/12 + 3/12 = 7/12
cups.

flour was used in total?
Answer: 13/42
Solution: Converting to common denominator 42: 1/6 = 7/42 and 1/7 = 6/42. Adding: 7/42 + 6/42 = 13/42
cups.
Challenge 10: Ethan used 3/5 of a cup of flour for cookies and 2/6 of a cup for a cake. How much
flour was used in total?
Answer: 28/30
Solution: Converting to common denominator 30: 3/5 = 18/30 and 2/6 = 10/30. Adding: 18/30 + 10/30 =
28/30 cups.
Challenge 11: Jack used 1/4 of a cup of flour for cookies and 3/5 of a cup for a cake. How much flour
was used in total?
Answer: 17/20
Solution: Converting to common denominator 20: 1/4 = 5/20 and 3/5 = 12/20. Adding: 5/20 + 12/20 =
17/20 cups.

Grade 6 Problems
Problem 1: Ethan has 4/2 of a pizza. They give 5/6 of that amount to a friend. What fraction of the
whole pizza did the friend get?
Answer: 20/12
Explanation: Multiplying fractions: (4/2) × (5/6) = 20/12 of the pizza.
Problem 2: Evelyn has 1/3 of a pizza. They give 5/7 of that amount to a friend. What fraction of the
whole pizza did the friend get?
Answer: 5/21
Explanation: Multiplying fractions: (1/3) × (5/7) = 5/21 of the pizza.
Problem 3: Elizabeth has 2/3 pounds of trail mix to divide into bags of 3/2 pounds each. How many
bags can be made?

Answer: 4/9
Explanation: Dividing fractions: (2/3) ÷ (3/2) = (2/3) × (2/3) = 4/9 bags.
Problem 4: Samuel has 2/5 of a pizza. They give 3/6 of that amount to a friend. What fraction of the
whole pizza did the friend get?
Answer: 6/30
Explanation: Multiplying fractions: (2/5) × (3/6) = 6/30 of the pizza.
Problem 5: Samuel has 3/3 of a pizza. They give 2/6 of that amount to a friend. What fraction of the
whole pizza did the friend get?
Answer: 6/18
Explanation: Multiplying fractions: (3/3) × (2/6) = 6/18 of the pizza.
Problem 6: Ethan has 3/8 of a pizza. They give 4/4 of that amount to a friend. What fraction of the
whole pizza did the friend get?
Answer: 12/32
Explanation: Multiplying fractions: (3/8) × (4/4) = 12/32 of the pizza.
Problem 7: Abigail has 1/3 of a pizza. They give 1/3 of that amount to a friend. What fraction of the
whole pizza did the friend get?
Answer: 1/9
Explanation: Multiplying fractions: (1/3) × (1/3) = 1/9 of the pizza.
Problem 8: Sophia has 5/6 pounds of trail mix to divide into bags of 1/6 pounds each. How many

Answer: 30/6
Explanation: Dividing fractions: (5/6) ÷ (1/6) = (5/6) × (6/1) = 30/6 bags.

Chapter 3: Geometry Basics
Geometry helps us understand shapes, sizes, and spatial relationships. Students will explore
perimeter, area, volume, and properties of various geometric figures through hands-on story
problems.
Grade 1 Problems
Problem 1: Henry draws 13 circles. How many corners do all the circles have in total? (Remember:
circles have 0 corners, triangles have 3, squares and rectangles have 4)
Answer: 0
Explanation: Each circle has 0 corners. 13 × 0 = 0 corners.
Problem 2: James draws 4 circles. How many corners do all the circles have in total? (Remember:
circles have 0 corners, triangles have 3, squares and rectangles have 4)
Answer: 0
Explanation: Each circle has 0 corners. 4 × 0 = 0 corners.
Problem 3: Sebastian draws 9 squares. How many corners do all the squares have in total?
(Remember: circles have 0 corners, triangles have 3, squares and rectangles have 4)
Answer: 36
Explanation: Each square has 4 corners. 9 × 4 = 36 corners.
Problem 4: Lucas draws 13 rectangles. How many corners do all the rectangles have in total?
(Remember: circles have 0 corners, triangles have 3, squares and rectangles have 4)
Answer: 52
Explanation: Each rectangle has 4 corners. 13 × 4 = 52 corners.
Problem 5: Avery draws 6 rectangles. How many corners do all the rectangles have in total?
(Remember: circles have 0 corners, triangles have 3, squares and rectangles have 4)
Answer: 24
Explanation: Each rectangle has 4 corners. 6 × 4 = 24 corners.

Problem 6: Sophia draws 13 rectangles. How many corners do all the rectangles have in total?
(Remember: circles have 0 corners, triangles have 3, squares and rectangles have 4)
Answer: 52
Explanation: Each rectangle has 4 corners. 13 × 4 = 52 corners.
Problem 7: Alexander draws 8 circles. How many corners do all the circles have in total?
(Remember: circles have 0 corners, triangles have 3, squares and rectangles have 4)

Explanation: Each circle has 0 corners. 8 × 0 = 0 corners.

(Remember: circles have 0 corners, triangles have 3, squares and rectangles have 4)
Answer: 12
Explanation: Each triangle has 3 corners. 4 × 3 = 12 corners.

Grade 2 Problems
Problem 1: Joseph has a rectangular garden that is 8 meters long and 4 meters wide. What is the
perimeter of the garden?
Answer: 24
Explanation: Perimeter = 2 × (length + width) = 2 × (8 + 4) = 2 × 12 = 24 meters.
Problem 2: Elizabeth has a rectangular garden that is 7 meters long and 4 meters wide. What is the
perimeter of the garden?
Answer: 22
Explanation: Perimeter = 2 × (length + width) = 2 × (7 + 4) = 2 × 11 = 22 meters.

Problem 3: Elizabeth has a rectangular garden that is 5 meters long and 4 meters wide. What is the
perimeter of the garden?

Problem 1: Lucas has a square playground with sides of 13 yards. What is the area and perimeter of
the playground?
Answer: Area: 169 sq yards, Perimeter: 52 yards
Explanation: For a square with side 13: Area = 13 × 13 = 169 square yards. Perimeter = 4 × 13 = 52
yards.

Grade 5 Problems
Problem 1: Aiden has a box that is 10 inches long, 4 inches wide, and 10 inches tall. What is the
volume of the box?
Answer: 400
Explanation: Volume = length × width × height = 10 × 4 × 10 = 400 cubic inches.
Problem 2: Jack has a box that is 8 inches long, 4 inches wide, and 10 inches tall. What is the

Grade 6 Problems

Problem 1: Alexander has a circular pond with a radius of 5 meters. What is the area? (Use π ≈ 3.14)
Answer: 78.5
Explanation: Area of circle = π × r2 = 3.14 × 52 = 3.14 × 25 = 78.5 square meters.
Problem 2: Abigail has a triangular garden with a base of 11 meters and height of 9 meters. What is
the area?
Answer: 49
Explanation: Area of triangle = (base × height) ÷ 2 = (11 × 9) ÷ 2 = 49 square meters.
Problem 3: Mason has a triangular garden with a base of 8 meters and height of 10 meters. What is
the area?
Answer: 40
Explanation: Area of triangle = (base × height) ÷ 2 = (8 × 10) ÷ 2 = 40 square meters.

Answer: 113.04
Explanation: Area of circle = π × r2 = 3.14 × 62 = 3.14 × 36 = 113.04 square meters.

area?
Answer: 27
Explanation: Area of triangle = (base × height) ÷ 2 = (6 × 9) ÷ 2 = 27 square meters.

Chapter 4: Ratios and Proportions
Ratios and proportions are powerful tools for comparing quantities. This chapter develops students'
ability to work with ratios, rates, and proportional relationships in practical contexts.
Grade 4 Problems
Problem 1: In David's class, the ratio of boys to girls is 4:2. If there are 12 students, how many are
boys?
Answer: 8
Explanation: The ratio 4:2 means 4 boys for every 2 girls. Total ratio parts: 6. Each part = 12 ÷ 6 = 2
students. Boys = 4 × 2 = 8.
Problem 2: In Emily's class, the ratio of boys to girls is 5:4. If there are 18 students, how many are
boys?
Answer: 10
Explanation: The ratio 5:4 means 5 boys for every 4 girls. Total ratio parts: 9. Each part = 18 ÷ 9 = 2
students. Boys = 5 × 2 = 10.
Problem 3: In Harper's class, the ratio of boys to girls is 3:5. If there are 16 students, how many are
boys?
Answer: 6
Explanation: The ratio 3:5 means 3 boys for every 5 girls. Total ratio parts: 8. Each part = 16 ÷ 8 = 2
students. Boys = 3 × 2 = 6.
Problem 4: In Alexander's class, the ratio of boys to girls is 3:3. If there are 12 students, how many
are boys?
Answer: 6
Explanation: The ratio 3:3 means 3 boys for every 3 girls. Total ratio parts: 6. Each part = 12 ÷ 6 = 2
students. Boys = 3 × 2 = 6.
Problem 5: In David's class, the ratio of boys to girls is 2:2. If there are 8 students, how many are

Answer: 4

Explanation: The ratio 2:2 means 2 boys for every 2 girls. Total ratio parts: 4. Each part = 8 ÷ 4 = 2
students. Boys = 2 × 2 = 4.

Challenge Problems - Grade 4
These advanced problems require multi-step thinking and deeper understanding.
Challenge 1: In Amelia's class, the ratio of boys to girls is 2:4. If there are 12 students, how many are
boys?
Answer: 4
Solution: The ratio 2:4 means 2 boys for every 4 girls. Total ratio parts: 6. Each part = 12 ÷ 6 = 2 students.
Boys = 2 × 2 = 4.
Challenge 2: In Ethan's class, the ratio of boys to girls is 3:4. If there are 14 students, how many are
boys?
Answer: 6

Boys = 3 × 2 = 6.

Answer: 47
Explanation: Speed = distance ÷ time = 142 ÷ 3 = 47 miles per hour.

Grade 6 Problems
Problem 1: If 2 apples cost $6, how much do 6 apples cost?
Answer: 18
Explanation: Set up proportion: 2/6 = 6/x. Cross multiply: x = (6 × 6) ÷ 2 = $18.
Problem 2: If 4 apples cost $5, how much do 9 apples cost?
Answer: 11

Challenge Problems - Grade 6
These advanced problems require multi-step thinking and deeper understanding.
Challenge 1: If 3 apples cost $4, how much do 8 apples cost?

Answer: 10
Solution: Set up proportion: 3/4 = 8/x. Cross multiply: x = (4 × 8) ÷ 3 = $10.
Challenge 2: If 5 apples cost $6, how much do 10 apples cost?
Answer: 12
Solution: Set up proportion: 5/6 = 10/x. Cross multiply: x = (6 × 10) ÷ 5 = $12.

Chapter 5: Word Problems
Word problems require students to apply multiple mathematical concepts and develop
problem-solving strategies. This chapter presents diverse scenarios that integrate various
mathematical operations and concepts.
Grade 1 Problems
Problem 1: Evelyn has 11 marbles. They find 2 more marbles, then give 2 marbles to a friend. How
many marbles does Evelyn have now?
Answer: 11
Explanation: Start with 11, add 2: 11 + 2 = 13. Then subtract 2: 13 - 2 = 11 marbles.
Problem 2: Sebastian has 11 marbles. They find 7 more marbles, then give 3 marbles to a friend.
How many marbles does Sebastian have now?
Answer: 15
Explanation: Start with 11, add 7: 11 + 7 = 18. Then subtract 3: 18 - 3 = 15 marbles.
Problem 3: Henry has 11 marbles. They find 7 more marbles, then give 3 marbles to a friend. How
many marbles does Henry have now?
Answer: 15
Explanation: Start with 11, add 7: 11 + 7 = 18. Then subtract 3: 18 - 3 = 15 marbles.
Problem 4: Mia has 11 marbles. They find 5 more marbles, then give 5 marbles to a friend. How
many marbles does Mia have now?
Answer: 11
Explanation: Start with 11, add 5: 11 + 5 = 16. Then subtract 5: 16 - 5 = 11 marbles.
Problem 5: Harper has 6 marbles. They find 3 more marbles, then give 5 marbles to a friend. How
many marbles does Harper have now?
Answer: 4
Explanation: Start with 6, add 3: 6 + 3 = 9. Then subtract 5: 9 - 5 = 4 marbles.

Problem 6: David has 8 marbles. They find 4 more marbles, then give 1 marbles to a friend. How
many marbles does David have now?
Answer: 11
Explanation: Start with 8, add 4: 8 + 4 = 12. Then subtract 1: 12 - 1 = 11 marbles.

Grade 2 Problems
Problem 1: Olivia buys a toy for $15 and a book for $2. They pay with $33. How much change does
Olivia get?
Answer: 16
Explanation: Total spent: $15 + $2 = $17. Change: $33 - $17 = $16.
Problem 2: Henry buys a toy for $11 and a book for $8. They pay with $44. How much change does
Henry get?
Answer: 25
Explanation: Total spent: $11 + $8 = $19. Change: $44 - $19 = $25.

Grade 3 Problems
Problem 1: Jack and 4 friends buy 6 snacks at $9 each. If they split the cost equally, how much does
each person pay?
Answer: 10
Explanation: Total cost: 6 × $9 = $54. Each person pays: $54 ÷ 5 = $10.
Problem 2: Isabella and 2 friends buy 5 snacks at $8 each. If they split the cost equally, how much
does each person pay?
Answer: 13
Explanation: Total cost: 5 × $8 = $40. Each person pays: $40 ÷ 3 = $13.
Problem 3: Harper and 3 friends buy 5 snacks at $11 each. If they split the cost equally, how much
does each person pay?
Answer: 13
Explanation: Total cost: 5 × $11 = $55. Each person pays: $55 ÷ 4 = $13.

does each person pay?
Answer: 10

Solution: Total cost: 7 × $8 = $56. Each person pays: $56 ÷ 5 = $11.
Challenge 15: David and 1 friends buy 5 snacks at $3 each. If they split the cost equally, how much
does each person pay?
Answer: 7
Solution: Total cost: 5 × $3 = $15. Each person pays: $15 ÷ 2 = $7.

Grade 5 Problems
Problem 1: A bicycle costs $74. The sales tax is 10%. What is the total price including tax?
Answer: 81
Explanation: Tax amount: $74 × 10% = $74 × 0.10 = $7. Total: $74 + $7 = $81.
Problem 2: A bicycle costs $44. The sales tax is 10%. What is the total price including tax?
Answer: 48
Explanation: Tax amount: $44 × 10% = $44 × 0.10 = $4. Total: $44 + $4 = $48.

Grade 6 Problems
Problem 1: David works 5 hours per day for 8 days, earning $13 per hour. After paying $23 in
expenses, how much money does David have?
Answer: 497
Explanation: Total earned: $13 × 5 × 8 = $520. After expenses: $520 - $23 = $497.
Problem 2: Sofia works 7 hours per day for 10 days, earning $9 per hour. After paying $34 in
expenses, how much money does Sofia have?

Chapter 6: Measurement
Measurement is fundamental to daily life. Students will practice converting units, calculating with
different measurement systems, and solving real-world problems involving length, weight, volume,
and time.
Grade 1 Problems
Problem 1: Ethan has a pencil that is 8 inches long and another that is 5 inches long. How much
longer is the longer pencil?
Answer: 3
Explanation: Difference: |8 - 5| = 3 inches.
Problem 2: Elizabeth has a pencil that is 3 inches long and another that is 11 inches long. How much
longer is the longer pencil?
Answer: 8
Explanation: Difference: |3 - 11| = 8 inches.
Problem 3: Samuel has a pencil that is 7 inches long and another that is 12 inches long. How much
longer is the longer pencil?
Answer: 5
Explanation: Difference: |7 - 12| = 5 inches.
Problem 4: Isabella has a pencil that is 9 inches long and another that is 12 inches long. How much
longer is the longer pencil?
Answer: 3
Explanation: Difference: |9 - 12| = 3 inches.
Problem 5: Benjamin has a pencil that is 10 inches long and another that is 4 inches long. How much
longer is the longer pencil?
Answer: 6
Explanation: Difference: |10 - 4| = 6 inches.

Problem 6: Emily has a pencil that is 7 inches long and another that is 4 inches long. How much
longer is the longer pencil?
Answer: 3
Explanation: Difference: |7 - 4| = 3 inches.

Chapter 7: Data and Graphs
Data analysis and graphing help us make sense of information. This chapter teaches students to
collect, organize, interpret, and present data using various graphs and statistical measures.

Grade 2 Problems
Problem 1: A class has 7 students who prefer cats and 4 who prefer dogs. How many more students
prefer cats?
Answer: 3
Explanation: Difference: |7 - 4| = 3 students.
Problem 2: A class has 8 students who prefer cats and 7 who prefer dogs. How many more students
prefer cats?
Answer: 1

Explanation: Difference: |8 - 7| = 1 students.

students prefer cats?

Grade 3 Problems
Problem 1: David scored 88, 66, 94, and 76 on four tests. What is the average score?
Answer: 81
Explanation: Average = (88 + 66 + 94 + 76) ÷ 4 = 324 ÷ 4 = 81.
Problem 2: Henry scored 89, 72, 72, and 98 on four tests. What is the average score?
Answer: 82
Explanation: Average = (89 + 72 + 72 + 98) ÷ 4 = 331 ÷ 4 = 82.
Problem 3: Aiden scored 99, 70, 82, and 89 on four tests. What is the average score?
Answer: 85
Explanation: Average = (99 + 70 + 82 + 89) ÷ 4 = 340 ÷ 4 = 85.
Problem 4: Alexander scored 80, 90, 73, and 62 on four tests. What is the average score?
Answer: 76
Explanation: Average = (80 + 90 + 73 + 62) ÷ 4 = 305 ÷ 4 = 76.
Problem 5: Aiden scored 76, 61, 100, and 90 on four tests. What is the average score?

Explanation: Average = (76 + 61 + 100 + 90) ÷ 4 = 327 ÷ 4 = 81.

Answer: 78
Explanation: Average = (73 + 86 + 93 + 62) ÷ 4 = 314 ÷ 4 = 78.

common value)?
Answer: 9
Explanation: The value 9 appears most frequently in the data.

Grade 6 Problems
Problem 1: Test scores were: 33, 37, 38, 43, 50, 57, 70. What is the range?
Answer: 37
Explanation: Range = highest - lowest = 70 - 33 = 37.
Problem 2: Test scores were: 26, 27, 43, 61, 67, 68, 78. What is the range?
Answer: 52
Explanation: Range = highest - lowest = 78 - 26 = 52.

Problem 3: Test scores were: 23, 24, 32, 42, 50, 58, 68. What is the range?
Answer: 45
Explanation: Range = highest - lowest = 68 - 23 = 45.
Problem 4: Test scores were: 32, 35, 48, 48, 50, 72, 78. What is the range?
Answer: 46
Explanation: Range = highest - lowest = 78 - 32 = 46.
Problem 5: Test scores were: 21, 31, 44, 49, 56, 61, 78. What is the range?
Answer: 57
Explanation: Range = highest - lowest = 78 - 21 = 57.
Problem 6: Test scores were: 23, 23, 31, 43, 48, 59, 64. What is the range?
Answer: 41
Explanation: Range = highest - lowest = 64 - 23 = 41.
Problem 7: Test scores were: 22, 27, 33, 37, 48, 57, 71. What is the range?
Answer: 49
Explanation: Range = highest - lowest = 71 - 22 = 49.
Problem 8: Test scores were: 26, 36, 36, 45, 45, 46, 61. What is the range?
Answer: 35
Explanation: Range = highest - lowest = 61 - 26 = 35.
Problem 9: Test scores were: 32, 45, 56, 57, 68, 75, 79. What is the range?
Answer: 47
Explanation: Range = highest - lowest = 79 - 32 = 47.

Chapter 8: Mathematical Vocabulary and Examples
Mathematical vocabulary is the language of mathematics. This chapter provides clear definitions,
examples, and applications of key mathematical terms that students encounter throughout their
learning journey.
Grade 1 Problems
Problem 1: Term: SUM
Answer: The answer when you add numbers together
Explanation: Example: 5 + 3 = 8 (8 is the sum)
Problem 2: Term: DIFFERENCE
Answer: The answer when you subtract numbers
Explanation: Example: 9 - 4 = 5 (5 is the difference)
Problem 3: Term: EQUAL
Answer: Having the same value
Explanation: Example: 3 + 2 = 5 means both sides are equal
Problem 4: Term: GREATER THAN
Answer: Larger in value
Explanation: Example: 7 > 3 means 7 is greater than 3
Problem 5: Term: LESS THAN
Answer: Smaller in value
Explanation: Example: 2 < 6 means 2 is less than 6
Problem 6: Term: ADDEND
Answer: A number being added
Explanation: Example: In 4 + 5 = 9, both 4 and 5 are addends

Problem 7: Term: COUNT
Answer: To find the total number
Explanation: Example: Count the apples: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5
Problem 8: Term: COMPARE
Answer: To see which is more or less
Explanation: Example: Compare 8 and 5: 8 is more
Problem 9: Term: ONES
Answer: The rightmost digit in a number
Explanation: Example: In 47, the 7 is in the ones place
Problem 10: Term: TENS
Answer: The second digit from the right
Explanation: Example: In 47, the 4 is in the tens place
Problem 11: Term: MORE
Answer: A larger amount
Explanation: Example: 10 is more than 7
Problem 12: Term: FEWER
Answer: A smaller amount
Explanation: Example: 3 is fewer than 8
Problem 13: Term: PAIR
Answer: Two items together
Explanation: Example: A pair of shoes means 2 shoes
Problem 14: Term: HALF
Answer: One of two equal parts
Explanation: Example: Half of 8 is 4

Problem 15: Term: DOUBLE
Answer: Two times a number
Explanation: Example: Double 5 is 10
Problem 16: Term: NUMBER LINE
Answer: A line showing numbers in order
Explanation: Example: 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5... on a line
Problem 17: Term: PATTERN
Answer: Something that repeats
Explanation: Example: 2, 4, 6, 8 is a pattern of even numbers
Problem 18: Term: SHAPE
Answer: The form of an object
Explanation: Example: Circle, square, triangle are shapes
Problem 19: Term: CORNER
Answer: Where two sides meet
Explanation: Example: A square has 4 corners
Problem 20: Term: SIDE
Answer: An edge of a shape
Explanation: Example: A triangle has 3 sides
Problem 21: Term: WHOLE
Answer: All of something
Explanation: Example: The whole pie, not just a slice
Problem 22: Term: PART
Answer: A piece of something
Explanation: Example: I ate part of the cookie

Problem 23: Term: GROUP
Answer: Things put together
Explanation: Example: A group of 5 pencils
Problem 24: Term: ORDER
Answer: Arrangement from smallest to largest
Explanation: Example: Put in order: 2, 5, 7, 9
Problem 25: Term: TALLY
Answer: A mark to count
Explanation: Example: Make tally marks: | | | | means 4
Problem 26: Term: BEFORE
Answer: Coming earlier
Explanation: Example: 5 comes before 6
Problem 27: Term: AFTER
Answer: Coming later
Explanation: Example: 8 comes after 7
Problem 28: Term: BETWEEN
Answer: In the middle of two numbers
Explanation: Example: 5 is between 4 and 6
Problem 29: Term: SUM
Answer: The answer when you add numbers together
Explanation: Example: 5 + 3 = 8 (8 is the sum)
Problem 30: Term: DIFFERENCE
Answer: The answer when you subtract numbers
Explanation: Example: 9 - 4 = 5 (5 is the difference)

Grade 2 Problems
Problem 1: Term: PRODUCT
Answer: The answer when you multiply numbers
Explanation: Example: 4 × 3 = 12 (12 is the product)
Problem 2: Term: EVEN NUMBER
Answer: A number divisible by 2
Explanation: Example: 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 are even numbers

Problem 3: Term: ODD NUMBER
Answer: A number not divisible by 2
Explanation: Example: 1, 3, 5, 7, 9 are odd numbers
Problem 4: Term: DIGIT
Answer: A single symbol used to make numbers
Explanation: Example: The number 47 has two digits: 4 and 7
Problem 5: Term: PLACE VALUE
Answer: The value of where a digit is in a number
Explanation: Example: In 25, the 2 is in the tens place
Problem 6: Term: REGROUP
Answer: To exchange 10 ones for 1 ten (or similar)
Explanation: Example: 16 + 8: regroup 14 ones as 1 ten 4 ones
Problem 7: Term: ARRAY
Answer: Objects arranged in rows and columns
Explanation: Example: 3 rows of 4 = 3 × 4 = 12
Problem 8: Term: SKIP COUNT
Answer: Counting by a number other than 1
Explanation: Example: Skip count by 5: 5, 10, 15, 20
Problem 9: Term: HUNDREDS
Answer: The third digit from the right
Explanation: Example: In 347, the 3 is in the hundreds place
Problem 10: Term: ESTIMATE
Answer: To make a reasonable guess
Explanation: Example: Estimate 48 + 33: about 50 + 30 = 80

Problem 11: Term: ROUND
Answer: To change to the nearest ten or hundred
Explanation: Example: Round 47 to nearest 10: 50
Problem 12: Term: EQUATION
Answer: A math sentence with an equals sign
Explanation: Example: 7 + 5 = 12 is an equation
Problem 13: Term: NUMBER SENTENCE
Answer: A complete math statement
Explanation: Example: 9 - 3 = 6 is a number sentence
Problem 14: Term: MENTAL MATH
Answer: Solving in your head without paper
Explanation: Example: 20 + 15 = 35 (done mentally)
Problem 15: Term: FACT FAMILY
Answer: Related addition and subtraction facts
Explanation: Example: 3+4=7, 4+3=7, 7-3=4, 7-4=3
Problem 16: Term: MEASUREMENT
Answer: Finding the size of something
Explanation: Example: Measure the pencil: 6 inches
Problem 17: Term: LENGTH
Answer: How long something is
Explanation: Example: The length of the table is 3 feet
Problem 18: Term: WIDTH
Answer: How wide something is
Explanation: Example: The width of the book is 8 inches

Problem 19: Term: CENTIMETER
Answer: A metric unit of length
Explanation: Example: A paperclip is about 3 centimeters
Problem 20: Term: METER
Answer: A metric unit equal to 100 centimeters
Explanation: Example: A doorway is about 2 meters tall
Problem 21: Term: INCH
Answer: A unit of length
Explanation: Example: A quarter is about 1 inch across
Problem 22: Term: FOOT
Answer: A unit equal to 12 inches
Explanation: Example: A ruler is 1 foot long
Problem 23: Term: TIME
Answer: When something happens
Explanation: Example: What time is it? 3:30
Problem 24: Term: HOUR
Answer: A unit of time (60 minutes)
Explanation: Example: School lasts 6 hours
Problem 25: Term: MINUTE
Answer: A unit of time (60 seconds)
Explanation: Example: The test takes 20 minutes
Problem 26: Term: MONEY
Answer: Coins and bills used to buy things
Explanation: Example: A dollar equals 100 cents

Problem 27: Term: CENT
Answer: A penny, 1/100 of a dollar
Explanation: Example: 1 cent is written as $0.01
Problem 28: Term: DOLLAR
Answer: 100 cents
Explanation: Example: 1 dollar = 100 cents = $1.00
Problem 29: Term: PRODUCT
Answer: The answer when you multiply numbers
Explanation: Example: 4 × 3 = 12 (12 is the product)
Problem 30: Term: EVEN NUMBER
Answer: A number divisible by 2
Explanation: Example: 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 are even numbers

Challenge 4: Term: DIGIT
Answer: A single symbol used to make numbers
Solution: Example: The number 47 has two digits: 4 and 7
Challenge 5: Term: PLACE VALUE
Answer: The value of where a digit is in a number
Solution: Example: In 25, the 2 is in the tens place
Challenge 6: Term: REGROUP

Answer: To exchange 10 ones for 1 ten (or similar)
Solution: Example: 16 + 8: regroup 14 ones as 1 ten 4 ones
Challenge 7: Term: ARRAY
Answer: Objects arranged in rows and columns
Solution: Example: 3 rows of 4 = 3 × 4 = 12
Challenge 8: Term: SKIP COUNT
Answer: Counting by a number other than 1
Solution: Example: Skip count by 5: 5, 10, 15, 20
Challenge 9: Term: HUNDREDS
Answer: The third digit from the right
Solution: Example: In 347, the 3 is in the hundreds place
Challenge 10: Term: ESTIMATE
Answer: To make a reasonable guess
Solution: Example: Estimate 48 + 33: about 50 + 30 = 80
Challenge 11: Term: ROUND
Answer: To change to the nearest ten or hundred
Solution: Example: Round 47 to nearest 10: 50
Challenge 12: Term: EQUATION
Answer: A math sentence with an equals sign
Solution: Example: 7 + 5 = 12 is an equation
Challenge 13: Term: NUMBER SENTENCE
Answer: A complete math statement
Solution: Example: 9 - 3 = 6 is a number sentence
Challenge 14: Term: MENTAL MATH

Answer: Solving in your head without paper
Solution: Example: 20 + 15 = 35 (done mentally)
Challenge 15: Term: FACT FAMILY
Answer: Related addition and subtraction facts
Solution: Example: 3+4=7, 4+3=7, 7-3=4, 7-4=3
Grade 3 Problems
Problem 1: Term: QUOTIENT
Answer: The answer when you divide numbers
Explanation: Example: 12 ÷ 3 = 4 (4 is the quotient)
Problem 2: Term: REMAINDER
Answer: What is left after division
Explanation: Example: 13 ÷ 4 = 3 R1 (1 is the remainder)
Problem 3: Term: FRACTION
Answer: Part of a whole
Explanation: Example: 1/2 means one part out of two equal parts
Problem 4: Term: NUMERATOR
Answer: The top number in a fraction
Explanation: Example: In 3/4, the numerator is 3
Problem 5: Term: DENOMINATOR
Answer: The bottom number in a fraction
Explanation: Example: In 3/4, the denominator is 4
Problem 6: Term: EQUIVALENT
Answer: Having the same value

Explanation: Example: 1/2 = 2/4 = 3/6 are equivalent fractions
Problem 7: Term: COMPARE FRACTIONS
Answer: Deciding which fraction is larger
Explanation: Example: 3/4 > 1/2
Problem 8: Term: UNIT FRACTION
Answer: A fraction with numerator of 1
Explanation: Example: 1/3, 1/4, 1/5 are unit fractions
Problem 9: Term: MIXED NUMBER
Answer: A whole number and a fraction
Explanation: Example: 2 1/2 means 2 and one-half

Answer: Space inside a 2D shape
Explanation: Example: A 3×4 rectangle has area of 12 square units

Answer: Distance around a shape
Explanation: Example: Square with side 5: perimeter = 20

Explanation: Example: A pitcher has a capacity of 2 liters

Explanation: Example: The mass of the book is 500 grams

Explanation: Example: The weight of the dog is 30 pounds

Explanation: Example: A paperclip has mass of about 1 gram

Answer: 1000 grams
Explanation: Example: A textbook might have mass of 1 kilogram

Answer: Lines that never meet
Explanation: Example: Railroad tracks are parallel

Grade 4 Problems
Problem 1: Term: FACTOR
Answer: A number that divides evenly into another
Explanation: Example: Factors of 12: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12
Problem 2: Term: MULTIPLE

Answer: The product of a number and another integer
Explanation: Example: Multiples of 5: 5, 10, 15, 20...
Problem 3: Term: PRIME NUMBER
Answer: A number with only 1 and itself as factors
Explanation: Example: 2, 3, 5, 7, 11 are prime
Problem 4: Term: COMPOSITE NUMBER
Answer: A number with more than two factors
Explanation: Example: 4, 6, 8, 9, 10 are composite
Problem 5: Term: PERIMETER
Answer: The distance around a shape
Explanation: Example: Rectangle 5×3: perimeter = 5+3+5+3 = 16
Problem 6: Term: COMMON FACTOR
Answer: A factor shared by two or more numbers
Explanation: Example: Common factors of 12 and 18: 1, 2, 3, 6
Problem 7: Term: GREATEST COMMON FACTOR
Answer: The largest common factor (GCF)
Explanation: Example: GCF of 12 and 18 is 6
Problem 8: Term: COMMON MULTIPLE
Answer: A multiple shared by two or more numbers
Explanation: Example: Common multiples of 3 and 4: 12, 24, 36
Problem 9: Term: LEAST COMMON MULTIPLE
Answer: The smallest common multiple (LCM)
Explanation: Example: LCM of 3 and 4 is 12
Problem 10: Term: DIVISIBLE

Answer: Can be divided evenly with no remainder
Explanation: Example: 20 is divisible by 4 (20 ÷ 4 = 5)
Problem 11: Term: DIVISIBILITY RULE
Answer: A shortcut to check if a number divides evenly
Explanation: Example: A number is divisible by 2 if it ends in 0,2,4,6,8
Problem 12: Term: SIMPLIFY
Answer: To reduce a fraction to lowest terms
Explanation: Example: Simplify 6/8 to 3/4
Problem 13: Term: IMPROPER FRACTION
Answer: Numerator is larger than denominator
Explanation: Example: 7/4 is an improper fraction
Problem 14: Term: CONVERT
Answer: To change from one form to another
Explanation: Example: Convert 5/4 to 1 1/4
Problem 15: Term: DECIMAL
Answer: A number with a decimal point
Explanation: Example: 0.5 = 1/2, 0.75 = 3/4
Problem 16: Term: TENTH
Answer: One part out of ten
Explanation: Example: 0.1 or 1/10
Problem 17: Term: HUNDREDTH
Answer: One part out of one hundred
Explanation: Example: 0.01 or 1/100
Problem 18: Term: DECIMAL POINT

Answer: The dot separating whole and decimal parts
Explanation: Example: In 3.25, the point separates 3 and 25
Problem 19: Term: PLACE VALUE (DECIMALS)
Answer: Value based on position after decimal
Explanation: Example: In 1.47, 4 is in tenths, 7 in hundredths
Problem 20: Term: LINE GRAPH
Answer: A graph using points connected by lines
Explanation: Example: Shows temperature change over time
Problem 21: Term: BAR GRAPH
Answer: A graph using bars to show data
Explanation: Example: Compares quantities using bars
Problem 22: Term: PICTOGRAPH
Answer: A graph using pictures to represent data
Explanation: Example: Each picture = 5 students
Problem 23: Term: DATA
Answer: Information collected
Explanation: Example: Survey data: favorite colors of students
Problem 24: Term: SURVEY
Answer: Asking questions to collect data
Explanation: Example: Survey: What is your favorite sport?
Problem 25: Term: SCALE
Answer: The numbers on a graph axis
Explanation: Example: The scale goes from 0 to 100 by 10s
Problem 26: Term: INTERVAL

Answer: The space between marks on a scale
Explanation: Example: The interval is 5 (0, 5, 10, 15...)
Problem 27: Term: PROTRACTOR
Answer: A tool to measure angles
Explanation: Example: Use a protractor to measure a 45° angle
Problem 28: Term: COMPASS
Answer: A tool to draw circles
Explanation: Example: Use a compass to draw a perfect circle

Grade 5 Problems
Problem 1: Term: DECIMAL
Answer: A number with a decimal point
Explanation: Example: 3.5, 0.75, 12.25 are decimals
Problem 2: Term: PERCENT
Answer: Per hundred
Explanation: Example: 50% means 50 out of 100
Problem 3: Term: AREA
Answer: The space inside a shape
Explanation: Example: Rectangle 4×6: area = 24 square units
Problem 4: Term: VOLUME
Answer: Space inside a 3D shape
Explanation: Example: Box 3×4×5: volume = 60 cubic units
Problem 5: Term: VARIABLE
Answer: A letter representing a number
Explanation: Example: In x + 5 = 12, x is the variable

Problem 6: Term: EXPRESSION
Answer: Numbers and operations without equals
Explanation: Example: 3x + 7 is an expression
Problem 7: Term: EVALUATE
Answer: To find the value of an expression
Explanation: Example: Evaluate 2x + 3 when x = 5: 2(5) + 3 = 13
Problem 8: Term: COEFFICIENT
Answer: The number multiplying a variable
Explanation: Example: In 5x, the coefficient is 5
Problem 9: Term: CONSTANT
Answer: A number that doesn't change
Explanation: Example: In x + 7, the constant is 7
Problem 10: Term: ORDER OF OPERATIONS
Answer: Rules for calculating (PEMDAS)
Explanation: Example: Do parentheses, then exponents, multiply/divide, add/subtract
Problem 11: Term: PARENTHESES
Answer: Grouping symbols ( )
Explanation: Example: Calculate (3 + 5) × 2 = 8 × 2 = 16
Problem 12: Term: CUBIC UNIT
Answer: A 1×1×1 cube for measuring volume
Explanation: Example: Volume in cubic inches, cubic feet, etc.
Problem 13: Term: RECTANGULAR PRISM
Answer: A box shape with 6 rectangular faces
Explanation: Example: A shoebox is a rectangular prism

Problem 14: Term: COORDINATE PLANE
Answer: A grid with x and y axes
Explanation: Example: Points like (3, 4) on the plane
Problem 15: Term: X-AXIS
Answer: The horizontal number line
Explanation: Example: The x-axis goes left and right
Problem 16: Term: Y-AXIS
Answer: The vertical number line
Explanation: Example: The y-axis goes up and down
Problem 17: Term: ORIGIN
Answer: The point (0, 0) where axes meet
Explanation: Example: The origin is the center of the plane
Problem 18: Term: ORDERED PAIR
Answer: Two numbers showing a location (x, y)
Explanation: Example: (2, 5) means 2 right, 5 up
Problem 19: Term: COORDINATE
Answer: One of the numbers in an ordered pair
Explanation: Example: In (3, 7), the coordinates are 3 and 7
Problem 20: Term: MEAN
Answer: The average of a set of numbers
Explanation: Example: Mean of 4, 6, 8: (4+6+8)÷3 = 6
Problem 21: Term: MEDIAN
Answer: The middle number when ordered
Explanation: Example: Median of 3, 5, 9: the median is 5

Problem 22: Term: MODE
Answer: The most frequent number
Explanation: Example: Mode of 2, 3, 3, 5: the mode is 3
Problem 23: Term: RANGE
Answer: Difference between highest and lowest
Explanation: Example: Range of 2, 5, 9: 9 - 2 = 7
Problem 24: Term: OUTLIER
Answer: A value far from the others
Explanation: Example: In 5, 6, 7, 25: the 25 is an outlier
Problem 25: Term: STEM-AND-LEAF PLOT
Answer: A way to organize data by place value
Explanation: Example: Shows tens in stem, ones in leaf
Problem 26: Term: LINE PLOT
Answer: Data shown with X marks above a number line
Explanation: Example: Each X represents one data point
Problem 27: Term: DISTRIBUTIVE PROPERTY
Answer: Multiply outside number by each inside
Explanation: Example: 3(x + 4) = 3x + 12
Problem 28: Term: COMMUTATIVE PROPERTY
Answer: Order doesn't matter in addition/multiplication
Explanation: Example: 3 + 5 = 5 + 3
Problem 29: Term: DECIMAL
Answer: A number with a decimal point
Explanation: Example: 3.5, 0.75, 12.25 are decimals

Problem 30: Term: PERCENT
Answer: Per hundred
Explanation: Example: 50% means 50 out of 100

Challenge Problems - Grade 5
These advanced problems require multi-step thinking and deeper understanding.
Challenge 1: Term: DECIMAL

Answer: A number with a decimal point
Solution: Example: 3.5, 0.75, 12.25 are decimals
Challenge 2: Term: PERCENT
Answer: Per hundred
Solution: Example: 50% means 50 out of 100
Challenge 3: Term: AREA
Answer: The space inside a shape
Solution: Example: Rectangle 4×6: area = 24 square units
Challenge 4: Term: VOLUME
Answer: Space inside a 3D shape
Solution: Example: Box 3×4×5: volume = 60 cubic units
Challenge 5: Term: VARIABLE
Answer: A letter representing a number
Solution: Example: In x + 5 = 12, x is the variable
Challenge 6: Term: EXPRESSION
Answer: Numbers and operations without equals
Solution: Example: 3x + 7 is an expression
Challenge 7: Term: EVALUATE
Answer: To find the value of an expression
Solution: Example: Evaluate 2x + 3 when x = 5: 2(5) + 3 = 13
Challenge 8: Term: COEFFICIENT
Answer: The number multiplying a variable
Solution: Example: In 5x, the coefficient is 5
Challenge 9: Term: CONSTANT

Answer: A number that doesn't change
Solution: Example: In x + 7, the constant is 7
Challenge 10: Term: ORDER OF OPERATIONS
Answer: Rules for calculating (PEMDAS)
Solution: Example: Do parentheses, then exponents, multiply/divide, add/subtract
Challenge 11: Term: PARENTHESES
Answer: Grouping symbols ( )
Solution: Example: Calculate (3 + 5) × 2 = 8 × 2 = 16
Challenge 12: Term: CUBIC UNIT
Answer: A 1×1×1 cube for measuring volume
Solution: Example: Volume in cubic inches, cubic feet, etc.
Challenge 13: Term: RECTANGULAR PRISM
Answer: A box shape with 6 rectangular faces
Solution: Example: A shoebox is a rectangular prism
Challenge 14: Term: COORDINATE PLANE
Answer: A grid with x and y axes
Solution: Example: Points like (3, 4) on the plane
Challenge 15: Term: X-AXIS
Answer: The horizontal number line
Solution: Example: The x-axis goes left and right
Grade 6 Problems
Problem 1: Term: RATIO
Answer: A comparison of two quantities

Explanation: Example: 3:4 means 3 to 4
Problem 2: Term: PROPORTION
Answer: Two equal ratios
Explanation: Example: 2/3 = 4/6 is a proportion
Problem 3: Term: INTEGER
Answer: Whole numbers including negatives
Explanation: Example: ...-2, -1, 0, 1, 2...
Problem 4: Term: ABSOLUTE VALUE
Answer: Distance from zero
Explanation: Example: |-5| = 5 and |5| = 5
Problem 5: Term: EXPONENT
Answer: How many times to multiply a number
Explanation: Example: 23 = 2 × 2 × 2 = 8
Problem 6: Term: BASE
Answer: The number being multiplied in a power
Explanation: Example: In 53, the base is 5
Problem 7: Term: POWER
Answer: A number written with an exponent
Explanation: Example: 102 is a power of 10
Problem 8: Term: SQUARED
Answer: Raised to the power of 2
Explanation: Example: 4 squared = 42 = 16
Problem 9: Term: CUBED
Answer: Raised to the power of 3

Explanation: Example: 3 cubed = 33 = 27
Problem 10: Term: SQUARE ROOT
Answer: A number that when squared gives the original
Explanation: Example: √16 = 4 because 42 = 16
Problem 11: Term: PERFECT SQUARE
Answer: The product of a number times itself
Explanation: Example: 25 is a perfect square (5 × 5)
Problem 12: Term: RATE
Answer: A ratio comparing different units
Explanation: Example: 60 miles per hour is a rate
Problem 13: Term: UNIT RATE
Answer: A rate with denominator of 1
Explanation: Example: $3 per pound
Problem 14: Term: PERCENT
Answer: Parts per hundred
Explanation: Example: 75% = 75/100 = 0.75
Problem 15: Term: PERCENTAGE
Answer: A number expressed as a percent
Explanation: Example: 25% of 80 = 20
Problem 16: Term: INEQUALITY
Answer: A math sentence with <, >, ≤, or ≥
Explanation: Example: x > 5 means x is greater than 5
Problem 17: Term: SOLUTION
Answer: A value that makes an equation true

Explanation: Example: x = 7 is the solution to x + 3 = 10
Problem 18: Term: SOLVE
Answer: To find the value of the variable
Explanation: Example: Solve for x: 2x = 14, x = 7
Problem 19: Term: ISOLATE
Answer: To get the variable alone on one side
Explanation: Example: Isolate x in x + 5 = 12 by subtracting 5
Problem 20: Term: INVERSE OPERATION
Answer: The opposite operation
Explanation: Example: Addition and subtraction are inverse operations
Problem 21: Term: RECIPROCAL
Answer: A fraction flipped upside down
Explanation: Example: Reciprocal of 2/3 is 3/2
Problem 22: Term: GCF
Answer: Greatest Common Factor
Explanation: Example: GCF of 12 and 18 is 6
Problem 23: Term: LCM
Answer: Least Common Multiple
Explanation: Example: LCM of 4 and 6 is 12
Problem 24: Term: CIRCLE
Answer: A round shape, all points equidistant from center
Explanation: Example: A wheel is a circle
Problem 25: Term: RADIUS
Answer: Distance from center to edge of circle

Explanation: Example: If radius is 5, diameter is 10
Problem 26: Term: DIAMETER
Answer: Distance across a circle through center
Explanation: Example: Diameter is twice the radius
Problem 27: Term: CIRCUMFERENCE
Answer: Distance around a circle
Explanation: Example: C = πd or C = 2πr
Problem 28: Term: PI (Π)
Answer: The ratio of circumference to diameter
Explanation: Example: π ≈ 3.14 or 22/7
Problem 29: Term: RATIO
Answer: A comparison of two quantities
Explanation: Example: 3:4 means 3 to 4
Problem 30: Term: PROPORTION
Answer: Two equal ratios
Explanation: Example: 2/3 = 4/6 is a proportion
